### 1.5 Code obfuscation versus code models

The utility side of the trade-off: what renaming, comment removal and other semantics-preserving transformations do to a model's ability to understand code and to find vulnerabilities.

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| NOIR: Privacy-Preserving Generation of Code with Open-Source LLMs | Khoa Nguyen, Khiem Ton, NhatHai Phan et al. | USENIX Security 2026 (per arXiv comments) | [arXiv:2601.16354](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16354) | [baseline] [related-work] Closest 2026 competitor on the 'protect proprietary code from the LLM provider' problem. It needs white-box access to an open model and targets generation, so it cannot be used with Claude/OpenAI/Gemini APIs; CodeKavach's black-box slicing + pseudonymisation is complementary. Cite in related work and borrow its embedding-inversion attacker as an extra leakage baseline. |
| A Systematic Study of Code Obfuscation Against LLM-based Vulnerability Detection | Xiao Li, Yue Li, Hao Wu et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2512.16538](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.16538) | [related-work] The single most relevant empirical study for the research question: it tells us which transformations are 'safe' for detection accuracy (mostly layout/renaming) and which are destructive (control-flow). CodeKavach reuses its taxonomy to define L2 transformations and replicates the protocol on its own benchmark. |
| When Names Disappear: Revealing What LLMs Actually Understand About Code | Cuong Chi Le, Minh V. T. Pham, Cuong Duc Van et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2510.03178](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.03178) | [related-work] Quantifies the utility cost CodeKavach should expect at L2 and motivates 'type-preserving' pseudonyms (e.g. keep role hints such as user_input_1, sql_query_2, or keep framework/API names) rather than opaque v1/v2 names. ClassEval-Obf is a ready-made robustness benchmark. |
| CodeCipher: Learning to Obfuscate Source Code Against LLMs | Yalan Lin, Chengcheng Wan, Yixiong Fang, Xiaodong Gu | arXiv preprint (ICLR 2025 submission on OpenReview; not in proceedings), 2024 | [arXiv:2410.05797](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.05797); [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=bIup4xWg9K) | [baseline] [related-work] The closest direct competitor to CodeKavach's privacy layer and a mandatory baseline in the paper. Differences to stress: CodeCipher needs white-box embeddings of a surrogate model and targets generation tasks; CodeKavach is provider-agnostic, deterministic, reversible and evaluated on vulnerability triage with an explicit attacker. |
| Robustness and Trade-offs for Code LLMs on Protected Code | Jin Wen, Yuejun Guo, Yujie Ma et al. | ISSRE 2026 (per arXiv comments) | [arXiv:2609.04220](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04220) | [related-work] Fresh (2026) evidence that frontier models can work directly on protected code, which is the premise of L2. Use its three-regime design when measuring triage accuracy at each privacy level. |
| The Code Barrier: What LLMs Actually Understand? | Serge Lionel Nikiema, Jordan Samhi, Abdoul Kader Kaboré et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2504.10557](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.10557) | [related-work] Feeds both axes of the study: description quality under obfuscation (utility) and deobfuscation success (attacker). Its finding that general-purpose models cope better guides model selection per privacy level. |
| How Does Naming Affect LLMs on Code Analysis Tasks? | Zhilong Wang, Lan Zhang, Chen Cao et al. | Journal of Software Engineering and Applications 17(11), 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2307.12488](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.12488) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design rule for the pseudonymiser: generate neutral, never misleading, names and keep them consistent across the slice. Also an early baseline number for the L2 accuracy drop. |
| Natural Attack for Pre-trained Models of Code (ALERT) | Zhou Yang, Jieke Shi, Junda He, David Lo | ICSE 2022 | [arXiv:2201.08698](https://arxiv.org/abs/2201.08698); [GitHub: soarsmu/attack-pretrain-models-of-code](https://github.com/soarsmu/attack-pretrain-models-of-code) | [related-work] Warns that identifier renaming alone can flip a vulnerability classifier's verdict. CodeKavach must therefore test verdict stability (same finding, original vs. pseudonymised) and report a flip rate, not just aggregate accuracy. |
| On the (Im)possibility of Obfuscating Programs | Boaz Barak, Oded Goldreich, Russell Impagliazzo et al. | Journal of the ACM 59(2) (CRYPTO 2001), 2012 | [doi:10.1145/2160158.2160159](https://doi.org/10.1145/2160158.2160159); [wisdom.weizmann.ac.il](https://www.wisdom.weizmann.ac.il/~oded/p_obfuscate.html) | [related-work] Keeps the paper honest: CodeKavach cannot claim semantic hiding of the logic it sends. The defensible claim is minimisation (send less) plus pseudonymisation (remove naming channel), measured empirically. |
| A Taxonomy of Obfuscating Transformations | Christian Collberg, Clark Thomborson, Douglas Low | Technical Report 148, University of Auckland, 1997 | [researchspace.auckland.ac.nz](https://researchspace.auckland.ac.nz/handle/2292/3491) | [related-work] Vocabulary for describing CodeKavach's transformations: L2 is a layout obfuscation (identifier scrambling, comment removal) with a secret key; 'potency vs. resilience' maps onto our 'human unreadability vs. attacker-model recovery rate'. |
| Protecting Software through Obfuscation: Can It Keep Pace with Progress in Code Analysis? | Sebastian Schrittwieser, Stefan Katzenbeisser, Johannes Kinder, Georg Merzdovnik, Edgar Weippl | ACM Computing Surveys 49(1), 2016 | [doi:10.1145/2886012](https://doi.org/10.1145/2886012) | [related-work] Standard survey of the obfuscation-versus-analysis arms race, organised by attacker goal (locating data, locating code, extracting code, understanding code) and analysis technique. Supplies the pre-LLM vocabulary for the attacker goals in the leakage model and supports the statement that identifier renaming is the weakest obfuscation class against a motivated analyst. |
| Acoda: Adversarial Code Obfuscation for Defending against LLM-based Analysis | Hongzhou Rao, Zikan Dong, Yanjie Zhao et al. | ICSE 2026 (arXiv June 2026) | [arXiv:2606.11755](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11755); [doi:10.1145/3774748.3793624](https://doi.org/10.1145/3774748.3793624) | [related-work] Opposite goal to CodeKavach (it wants the LLM to fail), so it marks the boundary of the design space: transformations Acoda finds effective are the ones CodeKavach must avoid, and its IP-leakage motivation supports our threat model. |
| Can LLMs Recover Program Semantics? A Systematic Evaluation with Symbolic Execution | Rong Feng, Suman Saha | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2511.19130](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.19130) | [inspiration] [related-work] Shows how a motivated attacker can augment an LLM with program-analysis artefacts, a realistic upper bound for the provider-side adversary in the CodeKavach threat model. |
| Deconstructing Obfuscation: A Four-Dimensional Framework for Evaluating LLMs' Assembly Code Deobfuscation Capabilities | Anton Tkachenko, Dmitrij Suskevic, Benjamin Adolphi | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2505.19887](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19887) | [related-work] Attacker-capability evidence: which obfuscation classes current LLMs can undo. |
| ObscuraCoder: Powering Efficient Code LM Pre-Training Via Obfuscation Grounding | Indraneil Paul, Haoyi Yang, Goran Glavaš et al. | ICLR 2025 | [arXiv:2504.00019](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.00019); [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=VYvxrD7aS0) | [related-work] Signals that future models will be explicitly trained to see through identifier obfuscation: good for CodeKavach's utility, bad for leakage. |
| Can LLMs Obfuscate Code? A Systematic Analysis of Large Language Models into Assembly Code Obfuscation | Seyedreza Mohseni, Seyedali Mohammadi, Deepa Tilwani et al. | AAAI 2025, 2024 | [arXiv:2412.16135](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.16135) | [related-work] Peripheral. Cite when discussing LLM-driven (rather than rule-driven) obfuscation as future work for an L2+ mode. |
| Adversarial Examples for Models of Code | Noam Yefet, Uri Alon, Eran Yahav | OOPSLA 2020, 2019 | [arXiv:1910.07517](https://arxiv.org/abs/1910.07517) | [related-work] Foundational robustness citation showing neural code models over-rely on names; complements ALERT. |

### 1.6 Attacker models: identifier recovery, deobfuscation, stylometry, memorisation

The leakage side of the trade-off: what an adversary who sees the sanitised payload can recover, how code can be re-identified, why code sent to a provider may resurface, and the metrics used to quantify all of this.

**Identifier recovery, deobfuscation and decompilation**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| DOBF: A Deobfuscation Pre-Training Objective for Programming Languages | Baptiste Roziere, Marie-Anne Lachaux, Marc Szafraniec, Guillaume Lample | NeurIPS 2021 | [arXiv:2102.07492](https://arxiv.org/abs/2102.07492); [GitHub: facebookresearch/CodeGen](https://github.com/facebookresearch/CodeGen) | [evaluation] [related-work] The most on-target learned attacker for source-level pseudonymisation: its training task is literally 'undo CodeKavach L2'. Fine-tune/evaluate it (and a prompted frontier LLM) as the primary identifier-recovery adversary. |
| JSNice: Predicting Program Properties from 'Big Code' | Veselin Raychev, Martin Vechev, Andreas Krause | POPL 2015 | [doi:10.1145/2676726.2677009](https://doi.org/10.1145/2676726.2677009); [jsnice.org](http://jsnice.org/) | [related-work] Canonical statistical attacker. Its result (most stripped names are predictable from context) is why CodeKavach must measure identifier recovery rather than assume renaming hides meaning. jsnice.org is still online for ad-hoc tests. |
| DIRTY: Augmenting Decompiler Output with Learned Variable Names and Types | Qibin Chen, Jeremy Lacomis, Edward J. Schwartz et al. | USENIX Security 2022 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity22/presentation/chen-qibin); [GitHub: CMUSTRUDEL/DIRTY](https://github.com/CMUSTRUDEL/DIRTY) | [evaluation] [inspiration] Reusable open-source attacker and dataset for C/C++; also the source of the standard metrics (exact match, character error rate) adopted in the CodeKavach leakage score. |
| DIRE: A Neural Approach to Decompiled Identifier Naming | Jeremy Lacomis, Pengcheng Yin, Edward J. Schwartz et al. | ASE 2019 | [arXiv:1909.09029](https://arxiv.org/abs/1909.09029) | [evaluation] [baseline] Attacker baseline for C/C++ and a cautionary tale for evaluation design: deduplicate and split by project so recovery rates are not inflated by train/test overlap. |
| Learning to Represent Programs with Graphs (VarNaming / VarMisuse) | Miltiadis Allamanis, Marc Brockschmidt, Mahmoud Khademi | ICLR 2018, 2017 | [arXiv:1711.00740](https://arxiv.org/abs/1711.00740); [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=BJOFETxR-) | [related-work] VarNaming is the source-level formulation of CodeKavach's L2 attacker: given pseudonymised code, guess the original identifier from usage context. Use the task definition and its accuracy/F1 scoring for the identifier-recovery leakage metric. |
| DeGuard: Statistical Deobfuscation of Android Applications | Benjamin Bichsel, Veselin Raychev, Petar Tsankov, Martin Vechev | ACM CCS 2016 | [doi:10.1145/2976749.2978422](https://doi.org/10.1145/2976749.2978422) | [evaluation] [baseline] Attacker baseline for Java/Kotlin. The library-identification result matters: even pseudonymised, a slice that embeds a recognisable library fingerprint reveals the stack. |
| CASCADE: LLM-Powered JavaScript Deobfuscator at Google | Shan Jiang, Pranoy Kovuri, David Tao, Zhixun Tan | ICSE-SEIP 2026, 2025 | [arXiv:2507.17691](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.17691) | [related-work] Proof that a large provider already runs production-grade LLM deobfuscation, grounding the 'provider as capable adversary' assumption. |
| Assessing LLMs in Malicious Code Deobfuscation of Real-world Malware Campaigns | Constantinos Patsakis, Fran Casino, Nikolaos Lykousas | Expert Systems with Applications (2024) | [arXiv:2404.19715](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.19715); [doi:10.1016/j.eswa.2024.124912](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.eswa.2024.124912) | [related-work] Real-world evidence that string/encoding obfuscation is weak against LLMs, so L1/L2 must remove or tokenise secrets rather than encode them. |
| DeGPT: Optimizing Decompiler Output with LLM | Peiwei Hu, Ruigang Liang, Kai Chen | NDSS 2024 | [NDSS](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/degpt-optimizing-decompiler-output-with-llm/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Prompt-only attacker recipe that needs no training: the same prompts can be pointed at a pseudonymised slice to see what meaning a provider could restore for free. |
| LLM4Decompile: Decompiling Binary Code with Large Language Models | Hanzhuo Tan, Qi Luo, Jing Li, Yuqun Zhang | EMNLP 2024 | [arXiv:2403.05286](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.05286); [GitHub: albertan017/LLM4Decompile](https://github.com/albertan017/LLM4Decompile) | [related-work] Shows how much lost structure LLMs can regenerate; relevant to L4 (IR-only) because an IR close to assembly can be lifted back to source-like code. |
| ReSym: Harnessing LLMs to Recover Variable and Data Structure Symbols from Stripped Binaries | Danning Xie, Zhuo Zhang, Nan Jiang et al. | ACM CCS 2024 (Distinguished Paper) | [doi:10.1145/3658644.3670340](https://doi.org/10.1145/3658644.3670340); [GitHub: lt-asset/resym](https://github.com/lt-asset/resym) | [related-work] State-of-the-art hybrid attacker (LLM + program analysis); represents the 'well-resourced provider' adversary tier. |
| VarBERT: 'Len or index or count, anything but v1' - Predicting Variable Names in Decompilation Output with Transfer Learning | Kuntal Kumar Pal, Ati Priya Bajaj, Pratyay Banerjee et al. | IEEE S&P 2024 | [doi:10.1109/SP54263.2024.00152](https://doi.org/10.1109/SP54263.2024.00152) | [related-work] Shows source-code pre-training transfers to name recovery, so any code LLM is a latent attacker. Include as a mid-size attacker model. |
| GenNm: Unleashing the Power of Generative Model in Recovering Variable Names from Stripped Binary | Xiangzhe Xu, Zhuo Zhang, Zian Su et al. | NDSS 2025 (arXiv title: 'Symbol Preference Aware Generative Models for Recovering Variable Names from Stripped Binary'), 2023 | [arXiv:2306.02546](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.02546); [NDSS](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/unleashing-the-power-of-generative-model-in-recovering-variable-names-from-stripped-binary/) | [evaluation] [related-work] Evidence that generative LLMs produce plausible unseen names, so CodeKavach's metric must include semantic similarity of recovered names, not only exact match. |
| code2vec: Learning Distributed Representations of Code | Uri Alon, Meital Zilberstein, Omer Levy, Eran Yahav | POPL 2019, 2018 | [arXiv:1803.09473](https://arxiv.org/abs/1803.09473); [GitHub: tech-srl/code2vec](https://github.com/tech-srl/code2vec) | [evaluation] [related-work] Lightweight attacker for function-name recovery and a historical anchor for the 'names are predictable from bodies' argument. |
| Context2Name: A Deep Learning-Based Approach to Infer Natural Variable Names from Usage Contexts | Rohan Bavishi, Michael Pradel, Koushik Sen | arXiv preprint, 2018 | [arXiv:1809.05193](https://arxiv.org/abs/1809.05193) | [evaluation] [related-work] Cheap neural attacker for the leakage metric; its usage-context formulation shows that the more context a slice contains, the more names leak, supporting L3 slicing. |
| JSNaughty: Recovering Clear, Natural Identifiers from Obfuscated JS Names | Bogdan Vasilescu, Casey Casalnuovo, Premkumar Devanbu | ESEC/FSE 2017 | [doi:10.1145/3106237.3106289](https://doi.org/10.1145/3106237.3106289) | [evaluation] [baseline] Second classical attacker baseline for JS/TS pseudonymised slices. |

**Code stylometry, authorship attribution and similarity-based re-identification**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| De-anonymizing Programmers via Code Stylometry | Aylin Caliskan-Islam, Richard Harang, Andrew Liu et al. | USENIX Security 2015 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity15/technical-sessions/presentation/caliskan-islam) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat-model input: even with names removed, syntactic style can link a slice to an individual developer or an organisation's house style. Motivates optional AST-normalising pretty-printing at L2 and a stylometric re-identification test. |
| I still know it's you! On Challenges in Anonymizing Source Code | Micha Horlboge, Erwin Quiring, Roland Meyer, Konrad Rieck | Proceedings on Privacy Enhancing Technologies 2024(3) | [arXiv:2208.12553](https://arxiv.org/abs/2208.12553); [doi:10.56553/popets-2024-0102](https://doi.org/10.56553/popets-2024-0102) | [evaluation] [related-work] The theoretical anchor for author-level leakage: generating a k-anonymous program is not computable in general, and in the authors' experiments code normalisation, coding-style imitation and obfuscation lower attribution accuracy but do not defeat an attacker who adapts to them. Its relaxed k-uncertainty measure is a candidate definition for the authorship metric in the leakage suite. Supports the CodeKavach position that the pretty-printing option is a measured reduction, not anonymisation, and that attribution must be evaluated with an attacker who knows the transformation. |
| Large-scale online deanonymization with LLMs | Simon Lermen, Daniel Paleka, Joshua Swanson et al. | USENIX Security 2026 | [arXiv:2602.16800](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16800); [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity26/presentation/lermen) | [evaluation] [inspiration] Template for the 'attacker LLM' in the leakage evaluation: feature extraction -> embedding retrieval -> LLM verification can be pointed at pseudonymised code slices to test whether the client or project can be re-identified. |
| I Can Find You in Seconds! Leveraging Large Language Models for Code Authorship Attribution | Soohyeon Choi, Yong Kiam Tan, Mark Huasong Meng et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2501.08165](https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.08165) | [evaluation] [related-work] Demonstrates that the same LLM receiving the slices can act as a stylometric attacker without any training: include an LLM-prompted re-identification probe in the leakage suite. |
| Misleading Authorship Attribution of Source Code using Adversarial Learning | Erwin Quiring, Alwin Maier, Konrad Rieck | USENIX Security 2019 | [arXiv:1905.12386](https://arxiv.org/abs/1905.12386) | [related-work] Catalogue of style-destroying yet behaviour-preserving transformations that CodeKavach can borrow for a 'style normalisation' option, with evidence that they defeat stylometry. |
| Large-Scale and Language-Oblivious Code Authorship Identification | Mohammed Abuhamad, Tamer AbuHmed, Aziz Mohaisen, DaeHun Nyang | ACM CCS 2018 | [doi:10.1145/3243734.3243738](https://doi.org/10.1145/3243734.3243738) | [evaluation] [related-work] Shows stylometric attacks survive simple obfuscation and scale; second attacker for the re-identification experiment. |

**Memorisation and training-data leakage**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| CodexLeaks: Privacy Leaks from Code Generation Language Models in GitHub Copilot | Liang Niu, Shujaat Mirza, Zayd Maradni, Christina Pöpper | USENIX Security 2023 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity23/presentation/niu) | [related-work] Concrete demonstration that code assistants leak identifiers, credentials and personal data seen in training; used to justify L1 redaction of secrets even for 'trusted' providers. |
| Traces of Memorisation in Large Language Models for Code | Ali Al-Kaswan, Maliheh Izadi, Arie van Deursen | ICSE 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2312.11658](https://arxiv.org/abs/2312.11658) | [inspiration] [related-work] Code-specific memorisation evidence for the threat model section. |
| Your Code Secret Belongs to Me: Neural Code Completion Tools Can Memorize Hard-Coded Credentials | Yizhan Huang, Yichen Li, Weibin Wu et al. | FSE 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2309.07639](https://arxiv.org/abs/2309.07639) | [related-work] Direct evidence for the bank scenario: hard-coded API keys in a repo can be memorised and replayed. Drives the rule that L1 is mandatory and cannot be disabled, even with self-hosted models that may later be fine-tuned. |
| Extracting Training Data from Large Language Models | Nicholas Carlini, Florian Tramer, Eric Wallace et al. | USENIX Security 2021, 2020 | [arXiv:2012.07805](https://arxiv.org/abs/2012.07805) | [related-work] Root citation for the risk that code sent to a provider which trains on customer data could later be regurgitated to third parties; underpins the requirement that CodeKavach defaults to no-training/ZDR endpoints and still minimises what is sent. |
| Does Your Neural Code Completion Model Use My Code? A Membership Inference Approach | Yao Wan, Guanghua Wan, Shijie Zhang et al. | ACM TOSEM 35(3), 2026 (arXiv 2024) | [arXiv:2404.14296](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.14296); [doi:10.1145/3742785](https://doi.org/10.1145/3742785) | [evaluation] [related-work] Method a client could use after an engagement to audit whether a provider trained on submitted slices; also one of the information-leakage metrics named in the project brief. |
| Unveiling Memorization in Code Models | Zhou Yang, Zhipeng Zhao, Chenyu Wang et al. | ICSE 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2308.09932](https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.09932) | [related-work] Supports the claim that unique proprietary logic, once trained on, is extractable; complements Al-Kaswan et al. |

**Information-leakage and similarity metrics**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Beyond Memorization: Violating Privacy via Inference with Large Language Models | Robin Staab, Mark Vero, Mislav Balunović, Martin Vechev | ICLR 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2310.07298](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.07298); [GitHub: eth-sri/llmprivacy](https://github.com/eth-sri/llmprivacy) | [evaluation] [related-work] Defines the attribute-inference attacker for the paper's leakage metric. Code analogue: ask an attacker LLM to infer business domain, organisation type, product and purpose of each slice, and score top-k accuracy against ground truth at L1-L4. |
| CodeBLEU: a Method for Automatic Evaluation of Code Synthesis | Shuo Ren, Daya Guo, Shuai Lu et al. | arXiv preprint, 2020 | [arXiv:2009.10297](https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10297); [GitHub: k4black/codebleu](https://github.com/k4black/codebleu) | [evaluation] Primary reconstruction-similarity metric: CodeBLEU(original, attacker reconstruction) per privacy level. The data-flow component should stay high for utility while the n-gram/name component drops; reporting the components separately is itself a contribution. |
| CodeBERTScore: Evaluating Code Generation with Pretrained Models of Code | Shuyan Zhou, Uri Alon, Sumit Agarwal, Graham Neubig | EMNLP 2023 | [arXiv:2302.05527](https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.05527); [GitHub: neulab/code-bert-score](https://github.com/neulab/code-bert-score) | [evaluation] [related-work] Embedding-similarity leakage metric for reconstructed code and recovered identifier names (semantic closeness rather than exact match). |
| Text Embeddings Reveal (Almost) As Much As Text (vec2text) | John X. Morris, Volodymyr Kuleshov, Vitaly Shmatikov, Alexander M. Rush | EMNLP 2023 | [arXiv:2310.06816](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06816); [GitHub: vec2text/vec2text](https://github.com/vec2text/vec2text) | [evaluation] [related-work] Warning for L4 and for any 'we only send embeddings/vectors' design: embeddings are not a privacy boundary. Usable as the inversion attacker for vector representations; check the SOM licence before bundling. |
| Language Model Inversion | John X. Morris, Wenting Zhao, Justin T. Chiu et al. | ICLR 2024, 2023 | [arXiv:2311.13647](https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.13647); [OpenReview](https://openreview.net/forum?id=t9dWHpGkPj) | [inspiration] [related-work] Relevant to the threat model for L4 and for logged outputs: anything derived from the prompt (logits, embeddings, responses) can leak it. |
| Information Leakage in Embedding Models | Congzheng Song, Ananth Raghunathan | ACM CCS 2020 | [arXiv:2004.00053](https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.00053) | [related-work] Attribute-inference framing for representations: even an abstract IR or embedding can reveal 'this is a payments ledger module'. |
| Membership Inference Attacks Against Machine Learning Models | Reza Shokri, Marco Stronati, Congzheng Song, Vitaly Shmatikov | IEEE S&P 2017, 2016 | [arXiv:1610.05820](https://arxiv.org/abs/1610.05820) | [evaluation] [related-work] Foundational citation for the membership-inference leakage metric named in the project brief. |
| BLEU: a Method for Automatic Evaluation of Machine Translation | Kishore Papineni, Salim Roukos, Todd Ward, Wei-Jing Zhu | ACL 2002 | [ACL Anthology](https://aclanthology.org/P02-1040/) | [baseline] [related-work] Baseline token-level similarity between the original code and the attacker's reconstruction; reported for comparability although it under-rates semantically equivalent code. |

**Network side channels on LLM traffic**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Whisper Leak: A Side-Channel Attack on Large Language Models | Geoff McDonald, Jonathan Bar Or | arXiv preprint (Microsoft), 2025 | [arXiv:2511.03675](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.03675) | [related-work] Same mitigation as for the remote keylogging attack (disable streaming or pad/batch responses); also an argument for air-gapped/self-hosted mode for the most sensitive clients. |
| What Was Your Prompt? A Remote Keylogging Attack on AI Assistants | Roy Weiss, Daniel Ayzenshteyn, Guy Amit, Yisroel Mirsky | USENIX Security 2024 | [arXiv:2403.09751](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.09751) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat-model input for hosted/CI deployments: a network observer can learn about findings from streamed responses. |

### 1.7 Program slicing, taint analysis and code property graphs

Algorithmic basis of the taint and slicing engine that decides which code fragment is a candidate for egress at privacy level L3.

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Modeling and Discovering Vulnerabilities with Code Property Graphs | Fabian Yamaguchi, Nico Golde, Daniel Arp, Konrad Rieck | IEEE Symposium on Security and Privacy (S&P) 2014 | [doi:10.1109/SP.2014.44](https://doi.org/10.1109/SP.2014.44); [cpg.joern.io](https://cpg.joern.io/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Foundation for CodeKavach's internal IR: the L3 'minimal vulnerability-relevant slice' and L4 'abstract-IR-only' levels are both projections of a CPG. Cite as the origin of the representation; reuse the open CPG schema (cpg.joern.io) rather than inventing a new one. |
| Program Slicing (Weiser) | Mark Weiser | IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering 10(4), 1984 | [doi:10.1109/TSE.1984.5010248](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.1984.5010248) | [inspiration] [related-work] Foundation of privacy level L3: CodeKavach's 'minimal vulnerability-relevant slice' is a backward slice from a sink (optionally intersected with a forward slice from a source). Cite as the formal basis and define the leakage metric over slice size. |
| FlowDroid: Precise Context, Flow, Field, Object-sensitive and Lifecycle-aware Taint Analysis for Android Apps | Steven Arzt, Siegfried Rasthofer, Christian Fritz et al. | PLDI 2014 | [doi:10.1145/2594291.2594299](https://doi.org/10.1145/2594291.2594299); [GitHub: secure-software-engineering/FlowDroid](https://github.com/secure-software-engineering/FlowDroid) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design reference for the source/sink/sanitiser model of CodeKavach's taint engine and an optional engine for Android/Java targets. LGPL-2.1: call it as a separate process or dynamically linked library, do not copy code into the MIT core. |
| Thin Slicing | Manu Sridharan, Stephen J. Fink, Rastislav Bodik | PLDI 2007 | [doi:10.1145/1250734.1250748](https://doi.org/10.1145/1250734.1250748) | [related-work] Theory for the L3 'minimal vulnerability-relevant slice': thin slices are the principled lower bound on what must be sent, with on-demand expansion mapping directly to the metered 'LLM asks for more context' loop. |
| Simplifying and Isolating Failure-Inducing Input (Delta Debugging) | Andreas Zeller, Ralf Hildebrandt | IEEE Trans. Software Engineering 28(2), 2002 | [doi:10.1109/32.988498](https://doi.org/10.1109/32.988498) | [related-work] Second minimisation stage for L3: after slicing, shrink the slice with ddmin using 'the local model / cached LLM verdict is unchanged' as the oracle, yielding an empirically minimal disclosure. |
| A Survey of Program Slicing Techniques | Frank Tip | Journal of Programming Languages 3(3), pp. 121-189 (also CWI report CS-R9438, 1994), 1995 | [franktip.org](https://www.franktip.org/pubs/jpl1995.pdf); [related page](https://www.franktip.org/pubs.html) | [related-work] Primary citation for the slicing background section and for choosing which slice definition L3 uses (backward static, dependence-graph based). More authoritative than the 2005 brief survey already listed. |
| Precise Interprocedural Dataflow Analysis via Graph Reachability (IFDS) | Thomas Reps, Susan Horwitz, Mooly Sagiv | POPL 1995 | [doi:10.1145/199448.199462](https://doi.org/10.1145/199448.199462) | [inspiration] [related-work] Algorithmic basis for CodeKavach's own taint engine; the reported taint path is a witness path in the exploded supergraph, which is what gets pseudonymised and sent at L3. |
| Interprocedural Slicing Using Dependence Graphs | Susan Horwitz, Thomas Reps, David Binkley | ACM TOPLAS 12(1), 1990 | [doi:10.1145/77606.77608](https://doi.org/10.1145/77606.77608); [doi:10.1145/24039.24041](https://doi.org/10.1145/24039.24041) | [inspiration] [related-work] Algorithmic basis for cross-function slices at L3; explains why naive call-graph expansion over-approximates and therefore leaks more code than necessary - directly relevant to the privacy-utility trade-off. |
| The Program Dependence Graph and Its Use in Optimization | Jeanne Ferrante, Karl J. Ottenstein, Joe D. Warren | ACM TOPLAS 9(3), 1987 | [doi:10.1145/24039.24041](https://doi.org/10.1145/24039.24041) | [related-work] Data structure behind the CodeKavach slicer (built from tree-sitter ASTs plus def-use and control-dependence edges). |
| SliceMate: Accurate and Scalable Static Program Slicing via LLM-Powered Agents | Jianming Chang, Jieke Shi, Yunbo Lyu et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2507.18957](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.18957) | [related-work] Shows the state of LLM-driven slicing, but the slicer itself needs to see the whole program, so in CodeKavach it could only run on a local model. |
| Taint-Based Code Slicing for LLMs-based Malicious NPM Package Detection | Dang-Khoa Nguyen, Gia-Thang Ho, Quang-Minh Pham et al. | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2512.12313](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.12313) | [related-work] Recent JS-specific confirmation that taint-guided slices beat whole-file prompts; its sensitive-API inventory is a useful seed for the JS/TS sink list. |
| NS-Slicer: A Learning-Based Approach to Static Program Slicing | Aashish Yadavally, Yi Li, Shaohua Wang, Tien N. Nguyen | Proc. ACM Program. Lang. 8 (OOPSLA1), 2024 | [doi:10.1145/3649814](https://doi.org/10.1145/3649814) | [related-work] Fallback for L3 when code does not parse or build (partial files, unsupported languages): a small local model can approximate the slice so that minimisation still applies. |
| Program Slicing in the Era of Large Language Models | Kimya Khakzad Shahandashti, Mohammad Mahdi Mohajer, Alvine Boaye Belle et al. | IEEE COMPSAC 2025 (arXiv 2024) | [arXiv:2409.12369](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.12369); [doi:10.1109/COMPSAC65507.2025.00155](https://doi.org/10.1109/COMPSAC65507.2025.00155) | [related-work] Justifies computing slices deterministically on the client rather than asking the (remote) LLM to find the relevant code, which would require sending everything. |
| LATTE: Harnessing the Power of LLM to Support Binary Taint Analysis | Puzhuo Liu, Chengnian Sun, Yaowen Zheng et al. | arXiv preprint (journal version in ACM TOSEM), 2023 | [arXiv:2310.08275](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.08275) | [related-work] Another slice-then-prompt precedent; its prompt-sequence construction along a call chain informs how CodeKavach serialises multi-function taint paths. |
| Stack graphs (name resolution at scale) | Douglas A. Creager, Hendrik van Antwerpen (GitHub) | Eelco Visser Commemorative Symposium (EVCS) 2023, 2022 | [arXiv:2211.01224](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.01224); [GitHub: github/stack-graphs](https://github.com/github/stack-graphs) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design inspiration for incremental, file-local name resolution without running a build; cite it, but do not depend on the archived crate. |
| PyCG: Practical Call Graph Generation in Python | Vitalis Salis, Thodoris Sotiropoulos, Panos Louridas et al. | ICSE 2021 | [arXiv:2103.00587](https://arxiv.org/abs/2103.00587); [GitHub: vitsalis/PyCG](https://github.com/vitsalis/PyCG) | [related-work] Reference algorithm for the Python part of CodeKavach's own slicing engine: tree-sitter gives no call resolution, and a PyCG-style assignment graph is the cheapest way to connect caller and callee functions when assembling an inter-procedural L3 slice. |
| Perses: Syntax-Guided Program Reduction | Chengnian Sun, Yuanbo Li, Qirun Zhang et al. | ICSE 2018 | [doi:10.1145/3180155.3180236](https://doi.org/10.1145/3180155.3180236); [GitHub: uw-pluverse/perses](https://github.com/uw-pluverse/perses) | [related-work] Matches CodeKavach's multi-language, tree-sitter-based design: reduce on the syntax tree so every candidate slice still parses. |
| QL: Object-oriented Queries on Relational Data | Pavel Avgustinov, Oege de Moor, Michael Peyton Jones, Max Schaefer (Semmle) | ECOOP 2016 (LIPIcs vol. 56) | [doi:10.4230/LIPIcs.ECOOP.2016.2](https://doi.org/10.4230/LIPIcs.ECOOP.2016.2); [GitHub: github/codeql](https://github.com/github/codeql) | [inspiration] [related-work] Cite in related work when positioning CodeKavach's abstract-IR (L4) questions and its own query layer; design inspiration for expressing taint specifications declaratively rather than in imperative Python. |
| C-Reduce: Test-Case Reduction for C Compiler Bugs | John Regehr, Yang Chen, Pascal Cuoq et al. | PLDI 2012 | [doi:10.1145/2254064.2254104](https://doi.org/10.1145/2254064.2254104); [GitHub: csmith-project/creduce](https://github.com/csmith-project/creduce) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design inspiration (and possible external tool) for aggressive slice reduction of C/C++ findings; its 'interestingness test' contract is the interface CodeKavach exposes for custom oracles. |
| All You Ever Wanted to Know about Dynamic Taint Analysis and Forward Symbolic Execution (but Might Have Been Afraid to Ask) | Edward J. Schwartz, Thanassis Avgerinos, David Brumley | IEEE S&P 2010 | [doi:10.1109/SP.2010.26](https://doi.org/10.1109/SP.2010.26) | [related-work] Serves as the taint-analysis survey/formalism reference; CodeKavach's taint policy configuration follows its three-part policy structure. |
| A Brief Survey of Program Slicing | Baowen Xu, Ju Qian, Xiaofang Zhang et al. | ACM SIGSOFT Software Engineering Notes 30(2), 2005 | [doi:10.1145/1050849.1050865](https://doi.org/10.1145/1050849.1050865) | [related-work] Background reference for the slicing chapter; amorphous slicing (any syntax-changing, semantics-preserving reduction) is the formal umbrella for 'slice then pseudonymise'. |
| Finding Security Vulnerabilities in Java Applications with Static Analysis | V. Benjamin Livshits, Monica S. Lam | USENIX Security 2005 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/14th-usenix-security-symposium/finding-security-vulnerabilities-java-applications-static) | [related-work] Historical grounding for web-application taint specifications used by the local deterministic stage. |

### 1.8 Confidential inference

Hardware-based and cryptographic alternatives to sanitisation. CodeKavach treats them as complementary deployment options, not as a replacement for data minimisation.

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Blueprint, Bootstrap, and Bridge: A Security Look at NVIDIA GPU Confidential Computing | Zhongshu Gu, Enriquillo Valdez, Salman Ahmed et al. | MLSys 2026 (per arXiv comments), 2025 | [arXiv:2507.02770](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02770) | [related-work] Caveat source: GPU confidential computing is young and partially opaque, so CodeKavach should treat TEEs as defence in depth on top of L1-L3, not a replacement. |
| A Survey on Private Transformer Inference | Yang Li, Xinyu Zhou, Yitong Wang et al. | arXiv preprint, 2024 | [arXiv:2412.08145](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.08145) | [related-work] One-stop citation for the cryptographic-inference paragraph of related work. |
| Confidential Computing on NVIDIA Hopper GPUs: A Performance Benchmark Study | Jianwei Zhu, Hang Yin, Peng Deng et al. | arXiv preprint (Phala Network technical report), 2024 | [arXiv:2409.03992](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.03992) | [related-work] Gives a citable overhead figure for the 'self-hosted open model inside a confidential GPU VM' deployment option, which is the alternative to pseudonymisation when a client can afford TEE hosting. |
| Confidential Prompting: Privacy-preserving LLM Inference on Cloud | Caihua Li, In Gim, Lin Zhong | arXiv preprint, 2024 | [arXiv:2409.19134](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.19134) | [related-work] Academic treatment of TEE-based prompt confidentiality to cite next to the vendor whitepapers. |
| NEXUS: Secure Transformer Inference Made Non-interactive | Jiawen Zhang, Xinpeng Yang, Lipeng He et al. | NDSS 2025, 2024 | [IACR ePrint](https://eprint.iacr.org/2024/136); [doi:10.14722/ndss.2025.230868](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2025.230868) | [related-work] Related work (HE inference); shows the direction of travel and the remaining performance gap. |
| PipeLLM: Fast and Confidential Large Language Model Services with Speculative Pipelined Encryption | Yifan Tan, Cheng Tan, Zeyu Mi, Haibo Chen | ASPLOS 2025 (per arXiv comments), 2024 | [arXiv:2411.03357](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.03357) | [related-work] Counterweight to the low-overhead Hopper GPU benchmark: TEE overhead depends on the workload. Cite when discussing why confidential inference alone is not a free substitute for prompt minimisation. |
| BOLT: Privacy-Preserving, Accurate and Efficient Inference for Transformers | Qi Pang, Jinhao Zhu, Helen Möllering et al. | IEEE S&P 2024, 2023 | [IACR ePrint](https://eprint.iacr.org/2023/1893) | [related-work] Related work (cryptographic private inference). |
| PUMA: Secure Inference of LLaMA-7B in Five Minutes | Ye Dong, Wen-jie Lu, Yancheng Zheng et al. | Security and Safety 4 (2025), EDP Sciences (arXiv 2023) | [arXiv:2307.12533](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.12533); [doi:10.1051/sands/2025014](https://doi.org/10.1051/sands/2025014) | [related-work] The headline number makes the point for the paper: cryptographic inference is orders of magnitude too slow for triaging hundreds of findings, which motivates the sanitise-and-minimise approach. |
| Iron: Private Inference on Transformers | Meng Hao, Hongwei Li, Hanxiao Chen et al. | NeurIPS 2022 | [proceedings.neurips.cc](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2022/hash/64e2449d74f84e5b1a5c96ba7b3d308e-Abstract-Conference.html) | [related-work] Related work for cryptographic private inference; cite to explain why MPC/HE is not yet practical for audit-scale LLM calls (minutes and gigabytes per query on BERT-sized models). |
| THE-X: Privacy-Preserving Transformer Inference with Homomorphic Encryption | Tianyu Chen, Hangbo Bao, Shaohan Huang et al. | Findings of ACL 2022 | [arXiv:2206.00216](https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.00216) | [related-work] Related work (HE inference). |
| Zero Knowledge Static Program Analysis | Zhiyong Fang, David Darais, Joseph P. Near, Yupeng Zhang | ACM CCS 2021 | [doi:10.1145/3460120.3484795](https://doi.org/10.1145/3460120.3484795) | [related-work] The cryptographic end of 'privacy-preserving code analysis': the owner of a secret program proves in zero knowledge that a public abstract-interpretation analysis (including a taint analysis) produces a given result, and the verifier never sees the code. Related work for L4 and for the privacy attestation; it proves the outcome of a fixed analysis and cannot supply the open-ended reasoning an LLM adds, which is the gap CodeKavach addresses. |
| Cheesecloth: Zero-Knowledge Proofs of Real World Vulnerabilities | Santiago Cuéllar, Bill Harris, James Parker, Stuart Pernsteiner, Eran Tromer | USENIX Security 2023 (journal version: ACM TOPS 28(4), 2025) | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity23/presentation/cuellar); [doi:10.1145/3747589](https://doi.org/10.1145/3747589) | [related-work] Proves in zero knowledge that a program contains a memory-safety or information-leak vulnerability without revealing the exploit. The mirror image of the CodeKavach setting (there the finder hides the vulnerability, here the owner hides the code); cite with Fang et al. as the formal alternative whose cost and narrow property classes motivate a measured, non-cryptographic approach. |

Document as a deployment option rather than a core dependency. |

Keeps the paper's claims about TEEs appropriately modest. |

| Azure AI Confidential Inferencing: Technical Deep-Dive | Microsoft Azure Confidential Computing team | Vendor technical blog, 2024 | [techcommunity.microsoft.com](https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azureconfidentialcomputingblog/azure-ai-confidential-inferencing-technical-deep-dive/4253150); [learn.microsoft.com](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/confidential-computing/confidential-ai) | [inspiration] [related-work] Reference architecture if CodeKavach offers a hosted tier with verifiable non-access; also a related-work data point that hyperscalers treat prompt confidentiality as a product requirement. |

### 1.9 Prompt injection and LLM-tool threats

The code under review is attacker-controlled text. These sources define the threat model for the LLM stage and for every integration that feeds model output into a client system.

**Prompt injection: attacks, defences and their limits**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Design Patterns for Securing LLM Agents against Prompt Injections | Beurer-Kellner et al. (multi-institution: IBM, Invariant Labs, ETH Zurich, Google, Microsoft and others) | arXiv, 2025 | [arXiv:2506.08837](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.08837) | [inspiration] [related-work] Architecture pattern: CodeKavach is an instance of 'LLM map-reduce' (each finding processed in isolation, outputs constrained to a schema, deterministic reduce) plus 'context-minimisation' (L3 slices). Cite to justify isolating findings from one another so one poisoned file cannot influence verdicts on others. |
| Defending Against Indirect Prompt Injection Attacks With Spotlighting (and Microsoft's defence-in-depth write-up) | Hines et al., Microsoft; MSRC blog by Andrew Paverd (29 July 2025) | arXiv preprint; Semantic Scholar lists the venue as CAMLIS (2024) | [arXiv:2403.14720](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.14720); [microsoft.com](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/msrc/blog/2025/07/how-microsoft-defends-against-indirect-prompt-injection-attacks) | [inspiration] [related-work] Integrate: wrap each code slice in randomised per-request delimiters, state that everything inside is data to be analysed and never followed, and optionally datamark comment/string tokens. Cheap, provider-agnostic, and measurable as an ablation. |
| Not What You've Signed Up For: Compromising Real-World LLM-Integrated Applications with Indirect Prompt Injection | Greshake, Abdelnabi, Mishra et al. | AISec 2023 (ACM CCS workshop) | [arXiv:2302.12173](https://arxiv.org/abs/2302.12173) | [inspiration] [related-work] Foundational citation for CodeKavach's threat model: every byte of the client's repository is untrusted input to the triage LLM. |
| CodeSentinel: A Three-Layer Defense Against Indirect Prompt Injection in Code Contexts | Po-Han Cheng, ..., Wei-Bin Lee | arXiv preprint (June 2026) | [arXiv:2606.19235](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19235) | [baseline] [inspiration] Design inspiration + baseline: architecturally the same place CodeKavach's privacy layer sits (tree-sitter pass over comments/strings/identifiers before the LLM). |
| Defeating Prompt Injections by Design (CaMeL) | Debenedetti et al., Google / Google DeepMind / ETH Zurich | arXiv, 2025 | [arXiv:2503.18813](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.18813) | [inspiration] [related-work] Architecture pattern: CodeKavach's triage model is a 'quarantined LLM' - it sees untrusted code, has zero tools, and its output is a typed value consumed by deterministic code. |
| OverThink and Sponge Examples: slowdown / energy-latency attacks | Kumar et al. (OverThink); Shumailov et al. (Sponge Examples, IEEE EuroS&P 2021) | arXiv; IEEE EuroS&P 2021, 2025 | [arXiv:2502.02542](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.02542); [arXiv:2006.03463](https://arxiv.org/abs/2006.03463) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model (OWASP LLM10 / denial-of-wallet): a hostile repository can contain decoy puzzles in comments, giant generated files or thousands of near-duplicate sinks. |
| The Attacker Moves Second (with: Lessons from Defending Gemini Against Indirect Prompt Injections) | Nasr, Carlini, ..., Tramer et al. (OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, ETH Zurich); Shi et al., Google DeepMind | arXiv, 2025 | [arXiv:2510.09023](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.09023); [arXiv:2505.14534](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14534) | [evaluation] [related-work] Evaluation rigour + honest claims: CodeKavach must not claim that a classifier or prompt wording 'prevents' injection; claims should rest on architecture (no tools, isolated findings, SAST findings cannot be silently suppressed) and any detector must be evaluated with adaptive attacks such as ALIBI. |
| Formalizing and Benchmarking Prompt Injection Attacks and Defenses | Liu, Jia, Geng et al. | USENIX Security 2024 | [arXiv:2310.12815](https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.12815) | [evaluation] [inspiration] Evaluate: reuse its attack taxonomy to generate the code-comment/string-literal injection corpus for CodeKavach's robustness experiments. |
| Securing AI Agents with Information-Flow Control (FIDES) | Costa, Kopf, Kolluri et al. | arXiv, 2025 | [arXiv:2505.23643](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23643); [GitHub: microsoft/fides](https://github.com/microsoft/fides) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design reference: CodeKavach already labels data (client code = confidential + untrusted). |
| Optimization-based Prompt Injection Attack to LLM-as-a-Judge (JudgeDeceiver) | Shi, Yuan, Liu et al. | ACM CCS 2024 | [arXiv:2403.17710](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.17710) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model: CodeKavach's triage step is an LLM judge over attacker-influenced text. |
| StruQ and SecAlign: structured queries and preference optimisation against prompt injection | Chen, Piet, Sitawarin, Wagner (StruQ); Chen et al. (SecAlign) | USENIX Security 2025 (StruQ); ACM CCS 2025 (SecAlign), 2024 | [arXiv:2402.06363](https://arxiv.org/abs/2402.06363); [arXiv:2410.05451](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.05451) | [related-work] Reference: relevant if CodeKavach later ships a fine-tuned local triage model - the fine-tune should include injection-resistant training on code slices. |
| The Instruction Hierarchy: Training LLMs to Prioritize Privileged Instructions | Wallace et al., OpenAI | arXiv, 2024 | [arXiv:2404.13208](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.13208) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design: place the rubric in the system/developer role and the untrusted slice in the lowest-privilege position the API offers (user content block or tool result), never concatenated into the system prompt. |

**Attacks on LLM code reviewers and vulnerability detectors**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ALIBI: Adaptive Agentic Attacks on LLM-Based Vulnerability Detectors via Adversarial Code Comments | Zixuan Wu and Cristina Nita-Rotaru | arXiv preprint (July 2026) | [arXiv:2607.24964](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24964) | [evaluation] [inspiration] Threat model + evaluate: the closest published attack to CodeKavach's exact setting. Directly motivates the L2+ design decision to strip or pseudonymise comments before the LLM sees them - turning the privacy layer into a security control - and gives an attack to replicate: measure attack success at L1 (comments intact) vs L2/L3 (comments removed). |
| Measuring and Exploiting Contextual Bias in LLM-Assisted Security Code Review | Dimitris Mitropoulos, Nikolaos Alexopoulos, Georgios Alexopoulos, Diomidis Spinellis | arXiv preprint, 2026 | [arXiv:2603.18740](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18740) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat-model input: commit messages, PR text and comments are attacker-controlled. CodeKavach's comment/string stripping at L2+ doubles as a defence; add a test case for it. |
| Syntax- and Compilation-Preserving Evasion of LLM Vulnerability Detectors (with: CoTDeceptor) | Sun, ..., Wong (2602.00305); Li et al. (2512.21250) | arXiv preprints, 2026 | [arXiv:2602.00305](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00305); [arXiv:2512.21250](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21250) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model + research angle: (1) LLM triage must never be able to *suppress* a deterministic SAST/taint finding without leaving an auditable trail. |
| Words Speak Louder Than Code: Investigating Cognitive Heuristics in LLM-Based Code Vulnerability Detection | Asif Shahriar, Hongyu Cai, Hadjer Benkraouda et al. | arXiv preprint, 2026 | [arXiv:2606.30587](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30587) | [evaluation] [inspiration] Prompt-hygiene rules: do not pass author names, prior tool verdicts or severity hints in ways that anchor the model; evaluate anchoring from SAST labels explicitly. |
| 'Your AI, My Shell': Demystifying Prompt Injection Attacks on Agentic AI Coding Editors | Yue Liu, ..., David Lo | arXiv preprint, 2025 | [arXiv:2509.22040](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.22040) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model for the VS Code extension and any agentic mode: CodeKavach's LLM step must have no shell, file-write or network tools; the model returns data only. |
| XOXO: Stealthy Cross-Origin Context Poisoning Attacks against AI Coding Assistants |  et al. | ACL 2026 (main), per arXiv comment, 2025 | [arXiv:2503.14281](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.14281) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model: context gathered from *other* files (callee bodies, configuration, docs) is attacker-influenced in multi-contributor repos; L3's minimal-slice policy reduces this surface and identifier pseudonymisation neutralises name-based steering. |
| ASCII Smuggler and Unicode Tag characters: invisible instructions for LLMs (Embrace The Red) | Johann Rehberger | 2024 | [embracethered.com](https://embracethered.com/blog/posts/2024/hiding-and-finding-text-with-unicode-tags/) | [related-work] Privacy-layer requirement: before pseudonymisation, normalise and strip Unicode Tag, bidi-control and zero-width characters from every identifier, string and comment that survives into a slice, and raise their presence as a finding in its own right (complements Trojan Source and the Rules File Backdoor entries). |
| Trojan Source: Invisible Vulnerabilities | Nicholas Boucher and Ross Anderson (University of Cambridge) | USENIX Security 2023, 2021 | [arXiv:2111.00169](https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.00169); [trojansource.codes](https://trojansource.codes/) | [related-work] Deterministic pre-check: flag and normalise bidi controls, zero-width characters and Unicode tag characters before parsing and before prompt construction - the same characters are used to hide prompt-injection text from humans (see Rules File Backdoor). |
| We Have a Package for You! Package Hallucinations by Code-Generating LLMs |  et al. | USENIX Security 2025, 2024 | [arXiv:2406.10279](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10279) | [inspiration] [related-work] Output-handling control: CodeKavach does not auto-fix, but remediation advice may name libraries; verify any package named in LLM output against the registry / the project's lockfile before it reaches a report. |

**Incidents and vulnerability research on AI code review and coding assistants**

| Item | Author / publisher | Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Clinejection: compromising Cline's production releases by prompting an AI issue triager | Adnan Khan | 2026 | [adnanthekhan.com](https://adnanthekhan.com/posts/clinejection/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model for the 'raise issues / Kanban cards' and GitHub App features: CodeKavach must never give an LLM tools in a workflow that an outsider can trigger, must not share Actions caches between untrusted-trigger and release workflows, and must keep publish tokens for its own VS Code extension and PyPI/npm packages out of any job an LLM or a PR can influence. |

| CamoLeak (GitHub Copilot Chat) and remote prompt injection in GitLab Duo (Legit Security) | Omer Mayraz, Legit Security | 2025 | [legitsecurity.com](https://www.legitsecurity.com/blog/camoleak-critical-github-copilot-vulnerability-leaks-private-source-code); [related page](https://www.legitsecurity.com/blog/remote-prompt-injection-in-gitlab-duo) | [inspiration] [related-work] Output-handling (LLM05) requirement: CodeKavach's dashboard, HTML/PDF reports, GitHub issues and VS Code webview must render model text as inert text - no images, no auto-linked URLs, no raw HTML, strict CSP - because rendered markup is an exfiltration channel for de-pseudonymised content. |
| How We Exploited CodeRabbit: from a simple PR to RCE and write access on 1M repositories (Kudelski Security) | Nils Amiet, Kudelski Security Research | 2025 | [kudelskisecurity.com](https://kudelskisecurity.com/research/how-we-exploited-coderabbit-from-a-simple-pr-to-rce-and-write-access-on-1m-repositories) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model (not an LLM bug, but CodeKavach's exact architecture): third-party analysers that honour in-repo configuration (RuboCop, ESLint, Semgrep rules, Gradle/Maven builds, pip installs for SCA) are code execution on the scanner host. |

(Checkmarx Zero) | Darren Meyer, Checkmarx Zero | 2025 | [checkmarx.com](https://checkmarx.com/zero-post/bypassing-claude-code-how-easy-is-it-to-trick-an-ai-security-reviewer/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model + test cases: seeds CodeKavach's adversarial test-suite (misleading comments, fake sanitiser wrappers, very long distracting comments). |
| GitHub Copilot agent-mode exploitation: CVE-2025-53773 (Embrace The Red) and Trail of Bits' injection engineering | Johann Rehberger; Trail of Bits (Kevin Higgs) | 2025 | [embracethered.com](https://embracethered.com/blog/posts/2025/github-copilot-remote-code-execution-via-prompt-injection/); [blog.trailofbits.com](https://blog.trailofbits.com/2025/08/06/prompt-injection-engineering-for-attackers-exploiting-github-copilot/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model for the VS Code extension: the extension must never write workspace settings or tasks, and model output must never be applied to files. Reinforces the project's 'no auto-fix' scope decision with concrete evidence. |
| GitHub MCP exploited: accessing private repositories via MCP (Invariant Labs) | Invariant Labs (now part of Snyk) | 2025 | [invariantlabs.ai](https://invariantlabs.ai/blog/mcp-github-vulnerability) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model for CodeKavach's MCP server and GitHub integration: scope tokens to a single repository per session, and make MCP tool results (finding text, snippets) clearly delimited data because the calling agent will treat them as context. |
| IDEsaster: a novel vulnerability class in AI IDEs | Ari Marzouk (MaccariTA) | 2025 | [maccarita.com](https://maccarita.com/posts/idesaster/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model for the VS Code extension: the extension must not write workspace settings, tasks or schema references on the LLM's say-so, and findings text returned by the model must be rendered as inert text (no command URIs, no auto-applied edits) - consistent with the 'no auto-fix' decision. |
| MCP tool poisoning attacks (Invariant Labs) and MCP Safety Audit | Invariant Labs; Radosevich and Halloran (arXiv 2504.03767) | 2025 | [invariantlabs.ai](https://invariantlabs.ai/blog/mcp-security-notification-tool-poisoning-attacks); [arXiv:2504.03767](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.03767) | [inspiration] [related-work] Threat model: CodeKavach as an MCP *server* must keep tool descriptions static and signed-off, and sanitise finding text (which contains attacker-controlled code/comments) before returning it, otherwise CodeKavach becomes the injection carrier into the developer's agent. |
| Rules File Backdoor (Pillar Security) | Pillar Security | 2025 | [pillar.security](https://www.pillar.security/blog/new-vulnerability-in-github-copilot-and-cursor-how-hackers-can-weaponize-code-agents) | [inspiration] [related-work] Detection rule + threat model: add an IaC/config-style check that scans AI-assistant instruction files (.cursor/rules, .github/copilot-instructions.md, AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md, .windsurfrules) for hidden Unicode and imperative payloads, and treat these files as hostile input if ever included in a slice. |

**Hostile-repository ingestion: archives, git and regular expressions**

The repository under review is attacker-controlled input to the scanner itself, before any LLM is involved. These sources back the ingest limits in `docs/research/INSIGHTS.md` section 7.

| Item | Author / publisher | Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A better zip bomb | David Fifield | USENIX WOOT 2019 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/woot19/presentation/fifield); [bamsoftware.com](https://www.bamsoftware.com/hacks/zipbomb/) | [inspiration] [related-work] Non-recursive zip files that expand by factors of tens of millions through overlapping entries, so a check for nested archives is not enough. Basis for the extraction limits on total uncompressed size, compression ratio and entry count when a client uploads an archive (CWE-409). |
| Zip Slip: arbitrary file write through archive extraction | Snyk Security Research | 2018 | [GitHub: snyk/zip-slip-vulnerability](https://github.com/snyk/zip-slip-vulnerability); [security.snyk.io](https://security.snyk.io/research/zip-slip-vulnerability) | [inspiration] [related-work] Disclosure of path traversal through crafted archive entry names (`../`), with a list of affected libraries across ecosystems. Basis for rejecting absolute paths, `..` segments and links that leave the extraction root (CWE-22, CWE-59), and a detectable pattern for the rule pack. |
| Git security advisory GHSA-8h77-4q3w-gfgv (CVE-2024-32002): recursive clones with submodules and symlinks lead to remote code execution | Git project | 2024 | [GitHub: git/git advisory](https://github.com/git/git/security/advisories/GHSA-8h77-4q3w-gfgv) | [inspiration] Shows that cloning an untrusted repository is itself an attack surface: a crafted submodule and symlink pair wrote a hook that ran during `git clone --recursive`. Basis for the rules that ingest never fetches submodules or LFS objects automatically, disables hooks and symlink checkout, runs inside the sandbox and pins a patched git version. |
| The Impact of Regular Expression Denial of Service (ReDoS) in Practice: An Empirical Study at the Ecosystem Scale | James C. Davis, Christy A. Coghlan, Francisco Servant, Dongyoon Lee | ESEC/FSE 2018 | [doi:10.1145/3236024.3236027](https://doi.org/10.1145/3236024.3236027) | [related-work] Finds super-linear regular expressions in a measurable share of npm and PyPI modules. Two uses: CodeKavach's own secret and rule patterns run on a linear-time engine or under a timeout because scanned files are hostile input (CWE-1333), and ReDoS is a finding class for client code. |

### 1.10 Secrets, PII, software composition, SBOM and IaC research

Research behind the local deterministic stages other than SAST.

**Secret detection**

| Paper | Authors | Venue/Year | Link | Relevance to CodeKavach |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A Comparative Study of Software Secrets Reporting by Secret Detection Tools (+ FPSecretBench) | Setu Kumar Basak, Jamison Cox, Bradley Reaves, Laurie Williams | ESEM 2023 | [arXiv:2307.00714](https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.00714); [GitHub: setu1421/FPSecretBench](https://github.com/setu1421/FPSecretBench) | [baseline] [related-work] Baseline numbers to compare CodeKavach's ensemble against, and justification for running several engines and unioning results for redaction. FPSecretBench is the test set for the LLM false-positive triage step. |
| How Bad Can It Git? Characterizing Secret Leakage in Public GitHub Repositories | Michael Meli, Matthew R. McNiece, Bradley Reaves | NDSS 2019 | [NDSS](https://www.ndss-symposium.org/ndss-paper/how-bad-can-it-git-characterizing-secret-leakage-in-public-github-repositories/); [doi:10.14722/ndss.2019.23418](https://doi.org/10.14722/ndss.2019.23418) | [inspiration] [related-work] Motivation and threat-model citation for L1 (secrets in client repos are the norm, not the exception) and the source of the 'distinctive token format + entropy filter' methodology that CodeKavach's pattern set follows. |
| Checked-In Secret Detection: Strings Are All You Need (StringGroup / Secretron) | Zhengdong Huang, Kevin Li, Jinqiu Yang et al. | ISSTA 2026 (per arXiv journal-ref) | [arXiv:2608.04523](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.04523) | [related-work] Directly relevant to the privacy-utility question: identifier pseudonymisation (L2/L3) is a form of obfuscation, and this work suggests string-literal context retains detection utility when identifiers are gone. |
| Secret Leak Detection in Software Issue Reports using LLMs: A Comprehensive Evaluation | Sadif Ahmed, Md Nafiu Rahman, Zahin Wahab et al. | MSR 2026 | [arXiv:2410.23657](https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.23657); [2026.msrconf.org](https://2026.msrconf.org/details/msr-2026-technical-papers/15/Secret-Leak-Detection-in-Software-Issue-Reports-using-LLMs-A-Comprehensive-Evaluatio) | [related-work] Relevant because CodeKavach itself writes GitHub issues and Kanban cards: evidence snippets posted to the client's repo must pass the same redactor. Also supplies comparative numbers for entropy vs. model-based detection. |
| Hey, Your Secrets Leaked! Detecting and Characterizing Secret Leakage in the Wild (KEYSENTINEL) | Jiawei Zhou, Zidong Zhang, Lingyun Ying et al. | IEEE S&P 2025 | [doi:10.1109/SP61157.2025.00122](https://doi.org/10.1109/SP61157.2025.00122); [GitHub: XingTuLab/KEYSENTINEL](https://github.com/XingTuLab/KEYSENTINEL) | [evaluation] [baseline] Top-venue 2025 baseline and second benchmark next to SecretBench for the L1 recall evaluation; the GPL-2.0 tool can only be run as an external comparison, not embedded. |
| Secret Breach Detection in Source Code with Large Language Models | Md Nafiu Rahman, Sadif Ahmed, Zahin Wahab et al. | ESEM 2025 | [arXiv:2504.18784](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.18784); [doi:10.1109/ESEM64174.2025.00021](https://doi.org/10.1109/ESEM64174.2025.00021) | [related-work] Closest prior work to CodeKavach's 'deterministic candidate -> LLM triage' loop for secrets. CodeKavach differs by never sending the secret value: the LLM sees a typed placeholder plus pseudonymised context. Use as a comparison baseline and for local-model sizing. |
| Automated Detection of Password Leakage from Public GitHub Repositories (PassFinder) | Runhan Feng, Ziyang Yan, Shiyan Peng, Yuanyuan Zhang | ICSE 2022 | [doi:10.1145/3510003.3510150](https://doi.org/10.1145/3510003.3510150) | [related-work] Passwords are the hard case for L1 redaction (no fixed format, low entropy). Motivates the context-aware detector (assignment to password-like identifier found via tree-sitter) in CodeKavach. |
| From Data Leak to Secret Misses: The Impact of Data Leakage on Secret Detection Models | Farnaz Soltaniani, Mohammad Ghafari | SANER 2026 | [arXiv:2601.22946](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22946); [doi:10.1109/SANER67736.2026.00078](https://doi.org/10.1109/SANER67736.2026.00078) | [evaluation] [related-work] Evaluation-methodology guard-rail: CodeKavach's benchmark splits (SecretBench, CredData) must be de-duplicated at repo and secret-value level before reporting ML/LLM triage accuracy. |
| Separating Secrets from Placeholders: A Hybrid CNN-CodeBERT Framework for Three-Class Credential Leakage Detection | Maksuda Bilkis Baby, Khushika Shah, Naiyue Liang, Lei Zhang | ICSME 2026 (accepted, per arXiv comment) | [arXiv:2605.31520](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31520) | [related-work] Supports a three-way label (real / placeholder / not a secret) in CodeKavach triage; placeholders should still be redacted at L1 but need not be reported as findings. |
| AssetHarvester: A Static Analysis Tool for Detecting Secret-Asset Pairs in Software Artifacts | Setu Kumar Basak, K. Virgil English, Ken Ogura et al. | ICSE 2025 | [arXiv:2403.19072](https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.19072); [doi:10.1109/ICSE55347.2025.00067](https://doi.org/10.1109/ICSE55347.2025.00067) | [inspiration] [related-work] Design inspiration for risk rating of CWE-798 findings: severity should depend on the protected asset, which CodeKavach can recover locally via data-flow from the literal to a connection call. |

Bissyandé, Jacques Klein | arXiv preprint (2025) | [arXiv:2510.18601](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.18601) | [related-work] Closest prior art for LLM-driven secret discovery in Java/Kotlin code - but it sends raw strings to the model. |

Klemmer, Nicolas Huaman et al. | USENIX Security 2023 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity23/presentation/krause); [arXiv:2211.06213](https://arxiv.org/abs/2211.06213) | [related-work] Human-factors citation for the report's remediation guidance (rotate first, then purge history) and for why audits must scan full git history rather than HEAD only. |

| Detection, Assessment and Mitigation of Vulnerabilities in Open Source Dependencies | Serena Elisa Ponta, Henrik Plate, Antonino Sabetta | Empirical Software Engineering 25, 2020 | [doi:10.1007/s10664-020-09830-x](https://doi.org/10.1007/s10664-020-09830-x); [GitHub: eclipse-steady/steady](https://github.com/eclipse-steady/steady) | [related-work] Methodological foundation for function-level reachability in CodeKavach's SCA triage. |

| CognixShield: PoV-Guided Vulnerable API Usage Detection in Large Codebases via LLMs (v1 title: SAVANT) | Quanzhi Fu, Wang Lingxiang, Wenjia Song et al. | ESEM 2026 (accepted, per arXiv comment) | [arXiv:2506.17798](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17798) | [related-work] Closest published analogue to CodeKavach's SCA triage step (send the calling slice, not the repo, to an LLM); cite for the approach and note that it does not address confidentiality of the code sent. |
| Uncovering Hidden Inclusions of Vulnerable Dependencies in Real-World Java Projects | Stefan Schott, Serena Elisa Ponta, Wolfram Fischer et al. | arXiv preprint (2026) | [arXiv:2601.23020](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23020) | [related-work] Supports a 'hidden/vendored dependency' check (hash or code-similarity based) and an explicit limitation statement in reports. |
| Does the Vulnerability Threaten Our Projects? Automated Vulnerable API Detection for Third-Party Libraries (VAScanner) | Fangyuan Zhang, Lingling Fan, Sen Chen et al. | IEEE TSE 50(11), 2024 | [arXiv:2409.02753](https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.02753); [GitHub: VAScanner/VAScanner](https://github.com/VAScanner/VAScanner) | [evaluation] [baseline] Source of vulnerable-API ground truth for Java and a baseline for CodeKavach's dependency-reachability experiments. |
| Backstabber's Knife Collection: A Review of Open Source Software Supply Chain Attacks | Marc Ohm, Henrik Plate, Arnold Sykosch, Michael Meier | DIMVA 2020 (LNCS) | [arXiv:2005.09535](https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.09535); [doi:10.1007/978-3-030-52683-2\_2](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-52683-2_2) | [related-work] Foundational citation for treating malicious packages as a finding class distinct from CVEs, and for install-script heuristics in the SCA stage. |
| Small World with High Risks: A Study of Security Threats in the npm Ecosystem | Markus Zimmermann, Cristian-Alexandru Staicu, Cam Tenny, Michael Pradel | USENIX Security 2019 | [USENIX](https://www.usenix.org/conference/usenixsecurity19/presentation/zimmerman) | [related-work] Background for the supply-chain section of the report and for treating maintainer/package health (OpenSSF Scorecard, deps.dev) as risk signals. |
